Norovirus on cruise ships: 10 reasons the virus moves so fast at sea

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Norovirus on cruise ships spreads easily through a tiny infectious dose, shared surfaces and close quarters — 10 things to know about outbreaks, tracking and how travelers can stay healthy

Norovirus does not need much help to make people sick. A trace of it on a fingertip, a fork or a stair railing can be enough to start an infection. Put that virus on a ship where passengers and crew share meals, hallways, elevators and entertainment for days. Each new case then gets many chances to become the next one.

That combination explains why norovirus on cruise ships draws so much attention. The virus causes sudden vomiting and diarrhea, and it moves through several routes at once. It travels on hands and shared objects. It can ride along in food and drinks. It also passes through close contact with someone who is sick.

A ship's population also changes with every voyage. New passengers arrive from many different places, while the crew stays aboard as guests come and go. Anyone can carry the virus on board without knowing it.

Cruise outbreaks are also unusually visible. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention runs a dedicated sanitation program for many cruise ships that call at U.S. ports. Those ships report gastrointestinal illness to the agency. The CDC publicly lists voyages whose illness counts cross its outbreak threshold. Most U.S. norovirus outbreaks happen elsewhere, in settings such as long-term care facilities. Those outbreaks rarely draw the same public scrutiny.

For travelers, the useful question is less whether norovirus exists at sea and more how it moves and what slows it down. Some familiar habits offer less protection than people assume. A clear picture of the virus's routes makes it easier to judge which precautions address which risks.

The 10 slides that follow break down the factors behind shipboard spread. They cover the biology of the virus, the layout and routines of a cruise ship and the way illness can board on embarkation day. They explain how long someone stays contagious, why hand hygiene method matters and how the CDC tracks outbreaks at sea. The final slides describe how crews respond when cases appear and how passengers can recover safely.

Norovirus needs very little to take hold. The CDC says as few as 18 virus particles can infect a person, an amount far too small to see, smell or taste.

That number is the starting point for understanding how the virus moves through a cruise ship. Infection does not require a large or obvious exposure. Contamination invisible to the eye can carry more than enough virus to cause illness.

The math works against prevention from the other side as well. The CDC notes that people sick with norovirus can shed billions of virus particles in their stool and vomit. One ill person can release far more virus than it takes to infect someone else. A small, unnoticed lapse in cleanup or handwashing can leave behind many times the infectious dose.

This gap between what a sick person releases and what a healthy person needs turns single cases into chains of infection. Each new case becomes another source of virus. On land, people disperse after an exposure to separate homes, offices and schools. On a ship, the same group of people stays together until the voyage ends. That gives each chain more chances to continue.

The low dose also explains why partial measures can fall short. A cleaning routine that removes most contamination may still leave enough behind to infect someone. Reducing the amount of virus on a hand or surface is not the same as removing it.

The CDC identifies norovirus as the leading cause of vomiting and diarrhea in the U.S. It is also the leading cause of foodborne illness in the country. Both rankings reflect the same trait. A virus that infects so readily has many ways to reach new hosts.

For cruise passengers, the practical lesson is that norovirus does not need a dramatic contamination event to spread. Routine contact with a small amount of virus is often enough. That is why the habits that limit exposure matter even when no one nearby appears sick.

Norovirus passes easily from person to person, and a cruise ship keeps passengers and crew in close contact in dining rooms, theaters, elevators and corridors for the length of a voyage. That repeated contact gives a single infection many chances to reach other people on board.

The CDC lists close contact with an infected person as one of the main ways norovirus spreads. Its examples include caring for someone who is sick and sharing food or eating utensils with them. On a ship, those situations play out among travel companions who share a cabin, a dinner table or a plate of appetizers.

Shared spaces also bring strangers into contact. Passengers line up for shows, sit close together in theaters, crowd into elevators between decks and gather in lounges and around pools. Shore excursions often put groups on the same buses and tender boats. Each gathering mixes people who would not otherwise meet.

The crew forms its own close community. Crew members typically share cabins, eat in common crew dining areas and work long shifts side by side. Many of them also serve food, clean cabins and staff guest areas. Their daily work connects the crew's living quarters with the passengers' spaces.

A ship is also a closed setting. On land, a person who feels unwell can go home and keep away from coworkers, classmates and strangers. At sea, a sick passenger stays inside the same community until the voyage ends. So does everyone that passenger spent time with in the days before.

Time adds to the risk. A multiday itinerary means the same group of people meets again and again — at breakfast, during activities and at dinner. An infection that starts early in a trip has days of ongoing contact in which to reach new people. Those new cases can in turn infect others while the voyage is still underway.

The result is a population that is dense, mixed and confined for the length of the trip.

Norovirus can stay infectious on hard surfaces, so a railing, elevator button or serving spoon can pass the virus from one passenger to the next. A person who touches a contaminated spot and then touches their mouth can become infected without ever being near a sick person.

That indirect route matters on a ship because so many hands land on the same objects. Passengers grip stairwell and deck railings to steady themselves as the vessel moves. Elevator call buttons and corridor door handles get pressed and pulled again and again over the course of a day.

Buffet utensils carry a similar risk. Tongs, ladles and serving spoons pass through a long line of hands during a single meal service. Each diner who picks one up touches the handle the previous diner just held. Many then go straight to eating.

Bathroom fixtures add another set of contact points. Faucet handles, flush levers, stall locks and exit doors are touched by people right after using the toilet. Norovirus is shed in stool and vomit. Hands that were not washed thoroughly can carry it onto the next surface they touch.

The infected person does not need to be present for this kind of spread. Particles left on a surface can be picked up by someone who arrives later. That gap in time and place makes the source hard to trace. Two passengers who never meet can share an illness through a single handrail.

Contaminated hands can also move the virus onward to new objects. A passenger who presses a contaminated button can transfer particles to a menu, a deck chair or a cabin key card.

Hand-to-mouth contact completes the route. People touch their faces often without noticing it. Eating, drinking and biting a fingernail all bring fingers to the lips. On a cruise, shared meals and snacks create many of those moments each day.

Norovirus spreads through food and drinks that an infected person has handled or that were contaminated before they reached the ship. On a cruise, large numbers of people eat from the same kitchens. A single lapse can reach far more diners than it would at a typical restaurant.

The CDC identifies norovirus as the leading cause of foodborne illness in the U.S. The virus does not need to multiply in food the way some bacteria do. It only needs to be present.

Contamination usually happens in one of two ways. The first is handling. A cook, server or bartender who is infected can transfer the virus to anything they touch. The risk is highest with food that will not be cooked afterward. Salads, sliced fruit, sandwiches, garnishes and desserts fall into this group. So does ice, which is scooped and served without any heating step.

The second route is contamination at the source. Some foods carry the virus before anyone on board touches them. Oysters and other shellfish harvested from polluted water are a common example. They are often eaten raw. Fresh produce can also pick up the virus during growing, harvesting or packing.

A ship's galley feeds passengers and crew across several dining rooms, cafes and bars. Much of that work draws on shared ingredients and shared prep areas. One contaminated batch or one ill food worker can therefore reach diners in very different parts of the vessel.

Drinks can carry the virus the same way. Drinking water and hand-mixed beverages can become contaminated at the source or by the person preparing them.

Food-related exposure is hard to separate from other routes. A passenger who falls ill after a meal may have been infected by the food itself. The cause could also be a person at the next table or something touched on the way in. That overlap makes it difficult to pin an outbreak at sea on a single source.

People with norovirus are most contagious while they are sick and during the first few days after they recover. The CDC says they can keep shedding the virus for two weeks or more after symptoms end. They can also spread it before they feel ill at all.

That timeline is a large part of why the virus is hard to contain in any group setting. A person stays contagious for longer than they feel sick. The people who pose a risk to others are not always the ones who look unwell.

The illness itself is short. Typical symptoms are vomiting, diarrhea, nausea and stomach pain. They usually appear 12 to 48 hours after exposure and tend to last one to three days.

The contagious window is a different matter. It can open before the first wave of nausea. Someone who was exposed may pass the virus along while they still feel fine. During that stretch, they have no reason to avoid shared spaces or to take extra care with handwashing.

Once symptoms start, a person is at their most infectious. Vomiting and diarrhea put large amounts of virus into the surrounding environment. Bathrooms, hands and anything those hands touch afterward can all become contaminated.

Recovery does not end the risk. A person who feels back to normal may return to eating with others, using shared restrooms and handling common items. The first days after recovery remain a period of high contagiousness, according to the CDC.

The tail end of shedding stretches much longer. That later shedding can continue well after a one- to three-day illness has passed. A person who has fully recovered can still carry the virus on their hands if they skip thorough washing after using the toilet.

On a ship, this timeline plays out in confined, shared quarters. A passenger can be contagious before they know they are sick. They are most infectious while unwell in their cabin. They may still be shedding the virus after they rejoin the dining room and other public areas.

For travelers, feeling better is not the same as being clear of the virus. Careful hand hygiene still matters for days after symptoms fade, not only while someone is visibly ill.

Norovirus usually reaches a cruise ship with the people who board it. A single passenger or crew member who walks up the gangway already infected can introduce the virus to a ship that was clean when the voyage began.

Embarkation day gathers people from many cities, states and countries into one terminal and one vessel. Each traveler arrives from a different community. Each brings whatever illnesses are circulating at home, at the airport or at the hotel where they stayed the night before.

Norovirus is common on land. The CDC identifies it as the leading cause of acute gastroenteritis in the U.S. Most U.S. norovirus outbreaks happen between November and April, according to the CDC. That means a winter or early spring sailing draws passengers from communities where the virus is more active.

Timing makes the problem harder to catch at the pier. Norovirus has an incubation period of 12 to 48 hours. A person exposed the day before departure can pass through check-in feeling well. Symptoms may not appear until the ship is already at sea.

By then, the infected traveler has spent hours in the terminal, in dining areas and in shared hallways. Anyone exposed during that time can fall ill a day or two later, while the ship is still sailing. That timeline is why illness on a cruise can first surface after departure rather than at boarding.

Crew members add a second path. Many crew members stay aboard across several consecutive voyages while passenger groups change around them. An infected crew member can carry the virus from one sailing into the next and expose an entirely new group of travelers.

The changeover between voyages also matters. The gap between one group leaving and the next arriving is short. That leaves limited time to clean cabins, restrooms and public areas thoroughly. Virus left on a surface that cleaners missed can reach the next group of passengers.

A single episode of vomiting can release norovirus particles into the air and scatter them across nearby surfaces. The CDC lists this among the ways the virus spreads. On a cruise ship, it can expose people who never touched the sick person or anything that person handled.

Norovirus illness often begins with sudden, forceful vomiting. That abrupt onset means a passenger may have no time to reach a bathroom. An episode can happen in a dining room, a theater line, an elevator or on a pool deck. Each of those spaces holds people who may be standing or sitting close by.

The particles do not stay in one spot. Some become airborne as tiny droplets that people nearby can swallow. Others settle onto tables, chairs, railings, floors and carpets around the incident. Anyone who later touches those surfaces and then their mouth faces a second round of exposure. That risk remains after the sick person has left the area.

Cabins carry their own version of this risk. A cabin bathroom is a small, enclosed room shared by everyone staying in that cabin. When one occupant vomits there, the toilet, sink, faucet handles, door handle and towels can all pick up the virus. Cabinmates then use the same fixtures throughout the day.

Bedding and clothing are another pathway. The CDC advises removing and washing any clothes or linens that may be soiled with vomit right away. It recommends handling those items carefully, without shaking them, and wearing rubber or disposable gloves while doing so. The CDC also calls for washing them with detergent and hot water at the longest available cycle. Machine drying on the highest heat setting should follow. Hands should be washed afterward.

For passengers, the practical response is distance. Moving away from a vomiting incident in a public area lowers the chance of swallowing airborne droplets. It also reduces contact with surfaces that may have been splashed. Inside a cabin, not sharing towels with a sick cabinmate removes one easy route for the virus to pass from one person to the next.

The CDC's main hand-hygiene advice against norovirus is to wash with soap and running water. The agency says alcohol-based hand sanitizers can be used in addition to handwashing, but they are not a substitute for it.

The gap comes down to how the virus is built. Norovirus is a non-enveloped virus. It lacks the fatty outer layer that alcohol breaks apart in viruses such as influenza and the coronavirus that causes COVID-19. Without that layer to attack, alcohol inactivates norovirus less reliably.

Handwashing works by a different mechanism. Lathering, scrubbing and rinsing physically lift virus particles off the skin and carry them down the drain. The CDC's general handwashing guidance calls for scrubbing for at least 20 seconds. That includes the backs of the hands, the spaces between fingers and the skin under the nails.

Sanitizer has a second weakness. The CDC notes that it may not work well when hands are visibly dirty or greasy. A quick pump after a buffet meal or a deck-chair snack can leave food residue on the skin, along with any virus mixed into it.

The CDC singles out two moments for handwashing: after using the toilet and before eating. Infected people can shed billions of norovirus particles in their stool and vomit, according to the CDC. Washing between the bathroom and the table cuts off a direct path from one person's illness to another person's mouth. On a cruise ship, meals and snacks come many times a day. Each one is another point where clean hands matter.

The same distinction applies to surfaces. For disinfecting contaminated areas, the CDC recommends chlorine bleach solutions at concentrations of 1,000 to 5,000 parts per million.

The U.S. Environmental Protection Agency keeps a list of registered disinfectants, known as List G, whose labels claim effectiveness against norovirus. A product that removes visible grime does not necessarily carry that claim, and it may leave the virus intact.

For passengers, the sanitizer dispenser at a restaurant entrance works best as a backup when no sink is nearby. Soap and water before meals and after bathroom visits remains the CDC's preferred method.

The CDC Vessel Sanitation Program monitors gastrointestinal illness on cruise ships. It publicly lists a voyage as an outbreak once 3% or more of passengers or crew report illness.

The program has a defined scope. It covers ships with foreign itineraries that carry 13 or more passengers and call at U.S. ports. A ship that never calls at a U.S. port falls outside it.

Reporting begins before a ship docks. Covered vessels send the CDC counts of gastrointestinal illness among passengers and crew ahead of their arrival at a U.S. port. Health officials receive the numbers while the voyage is still under way. That timing lets the agency see a problem before passengers walk off the ship.

The 3% threshold is what moves a cluster of sick travelers onto the CDC's public outbreak list. The bar can be met by passengers or by crew. On a voyage carrying 2,000 passengers, 60 ill passengers would reach it. In a crew of 1,000, 30 sick crew members would do the same. A smaller number of cases still counts as illness, but it does not trigger a public outbreak posting.

Inspections are the program's second tool. Ships receive sanitation inspection scores out of 100, and a score of 86 or higher is satisfactory. The score reflects conditions on the day inspectors visit. It measures something different from the illness counts a ship reports on each voyage. A ship can pass inspection and still have an outbreak on a later voyage.

The program's reporting rules also shape how often cruise outbreaks make news. Every covered voyage feeds into that system, and outbreaks that cross the threshold are posted publicly. Cruise ships account for only a small share of norovirus illness in the U.S. Their outbreaks surface quickly because the reporting system is built to catch them.

Travelers $TRV can check the records themselves. The CDC posts its outbreak list and ship inspection scores online. Anyone can look up a vessel's recent sanitation history before booking a cruise.

When norovirus breaks out on a cruise, ships typically ask sick passengers to stay in their cabins and step up disinfection across the vessel. Travelers $TRV can help contain it by reporting symptoms quickly, keeping hands away from the face and staying apart from others until at least 48 hours after symptoms end.

Cabin isolation works on a simple principle. A sick passenger who stays out of dining rooms, lounges and theaters cannot leave the virus on shared seats, tables or railings. Isolation also keeps that person out of the crowded lines and close conversations where person-to-person spread happens.

Stepped-up disinfection targets the other half of the problem. The work concentrates on the places many hands touch over the course of a day at sea. The aim is to remove virus that has already left an infected person before someone else picks it up.

Prompt reporting matters because a ship can only respond to illness it knows about. A passenger who tells the onboard medical staff about vomiting or diarrhea early gives the crew time to isolate the case. It also lets the crew clean the affected areas sooner. A passenger who hides symptoms keeps moving through shared spaces while contagious.

The 48-hour benchmark comes from CDC guidance on staying away from others after recovery. On a ship, following it can mean skipping excursions, shows and group meals even after a passenger feels better. Feeling well again is not the same as no longer being contagious.

Healthy passengers have their own part to play. Washing with soap and water before meals lowers the chance that a stray particle gets inside the body.

Treatment is supportive. There is no approved norovirus vaccine in the U.S. That means there is no shot to get before boarding. Care centers on drinking fluids to replace what the body loses and to prevent dehydration.

The nickname stomach flu causes confusion. Norovirus is not influenza, and a seasonal flu shot offers no protection against it.

How to prevent seasickness: 10 options and the cautions that apply

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

A guide to 10 seasickness remedies, from scopolamine patches to Sea-Band wristbands, covering what each option does, when to take it and which cautions apply

Seasickness starts with a disagreement inside the body. The inner ear senses a deck rising, falling and tilting. The eyes, fixed on a cabin wall or a phone screen, report that nothing is moving. The brain receives two conflicting signals about motion, and nausea, sweating, dizziness and fatigue can follow. That mismatch sits at the root of motion sickness at sea.

The condition affects cruise passengers, ferry riders, anglers and sailors alike. It can turn a planned trip into hours of discomfort. It also has a practical side. A traveler who is sick at sea may struggle to eat, drink, sleep or move around a vessel safely.

Seasickness remedies cover a wide range of approaches. Some are medications: a prescription patch worn behind the ear, and over-the-counter antihistamines sold under brand names such as Dramamine and Bonine. Others are drug-free, including acupressure wristbands and ginger. A third group involves behavior rather than products. That group covers where a passenger sits, what they eat and drink, when they take medication and how the body adapts to motion over time.

These options do not carry equal weight. The medications have defined labels, known side effects and specific cautions for certain health conditions. The evidence behind wristbands and ginger is mixed, and neither is a reliable fix. No single remedy cures motion sickness at sea or works for everyone.

Timing matters too. Most medical options are meant to be started before symptoms appear. That often means making a decision on land, not after the first wave of queasiness.

The 10 entries below are a selection, not a ranking or a complete list. Each slide explains what a remedy or strategy is, how it is used and what limits or warnings apply. The final slide covers when a doctor or pharmacist should be involved, including for travelers who are pregnant, older, very young or taking other medications. Whether the trip is a short harbor crossing or a weeklong cruise, knowing how to prevent seasickness starts with understanding which tools exist and what each one can do.

The scopolamine patch, sold in the U.S. as Transderm Scop, is a prescription option worn on the skin behind the ear. One patch is labeled to last up to three days. It is generally applied several hours before a trip begins.

The patch delivers scopolamine through the skin rather than through the stomach. The drug passes gradually into the bloodstream for as long as the patch stays on. That steady release is the main difference from a pill, which must be swallowed and taken again as each dose wears off.

Scopolamine belongs to a class of drugs called anticholinergics. It blocks acetylcholine, a chemical messenger the nervous system uses to relay motion signals from the inner ear. Outside the U.S., the same compound is often known as hyoscine.

The three-day span fits multi-day sailings and cruises. A single patch can cover a long stretch at sea without the need to track repeat doses. The patch requires a prescription. Getting one means seeing a doctor before departure.

Placement follows specific directions. The label tells users to press the patch onto clean, dry, hairless skin behind one ear. Hands should be washed after handling it. Scopolamine carried from the fingers to the eyes can widen the pupils and blur vision. After removal, the label directs users to wash both hands and the skin where the patch sat.

The patch has known side effects. Dry mouth and drowsiness are among them. The product label also warns that some users experience blurred vision or urinary retention, which means difficulty emptying the bladder.

Drowsiness carries practical weight on a boat. Anyone who needs to stay alert on deck, handle equipment or drive after docking should plan around it.

The patch is a prevention tool, not a cure. It does not stop seasickness in every user, and its effects vary from one person to the next.

Dimenhydrinate, sold as the original Dramamine formula, is an over-the-counter antihistamine taken before departure to help prevent seasickness. Drowsiness is its most common side effect and the main tradeoff for anyone who uses it.

The drug combines two substances. One is diphenhydramine, an older antihistamine. The other is 8-chlorotheophylline, a mild stimulant chemically related to caffeine. The diphenhydramine portion does most of the work against nausea and dizziness.

Older antihistamines of this type act on the brain, not only on the body. They dampen the brain's response to motion signals from the balance organs of the inner ear. That central action is what makes dimenhydrinate useful on a rolling deck. It is also the reason many people feel sleepy after taking it.

The first dose goes down before the boat leaves the dock. Further doses follow at set intervals. The package label states how often to repeat a dose and the maximum number of doses in a single day. Those limits differ by age. Each traveler in a group, adult or child, follows the label directions for their own age.

Sedation shapes where the drug fits into a trip. The Drug Facts label advises caution when driving a motor vehicle or operating machinery. That caution applies to anyone planning to steer a small boat, handle lines on deck or drive home from the marina after landing.

The label also notes that alcohol, sedatives and tranquilizers can increase drowsiness. A drink from the onboard bar can deepen the sleepiness the tablet already causes. Some users, especially children, become excitable instead of drowsy. The label lists that reaction as a possibility.

Diphenhydramine is also the active ingredient in some sleep aids and allergy medicines. Taking one of those alongside dimenhydrinate stacks a second dose of the same drug. Dry mouth is another common effect of this class of antihistamine.

For some travelers, the drowsiness is not a drawback. On an overnight ferry crossing, a passenger who sleeps through rough water may notice little of it. On a day of snorkeling, fishing or sightseeing, the same sedation can cut into the activity the trip was planned around.

Dimenhydrinate is sold as regular tablets and as chewable versions, including formulations made for children.

Meclizine is an over-the-counter antihistamine for motion sickness that is generally described as less sedating than dimenhydrinate. It is typically taken about an hour before travel, and a single dose is labeled to last up to 24 hours.

The drug reaches shoppers under more than one name. Bonine and Dramamine Less Drowsy both list meclizine as the active ingredient. Generic store-brand versions sit on many pharmacy shelves too. The Drug Facts panel is the simplest way to tell a meclizine product from one built on dimenhydrinate. That check matters because the two drugs can share a brand family and similar packaging.

Meclizine works on the vestibular system, the inner-ear network that tracks balance and motion. It blocks histamine receptors involved in relaying signals from that system to the brain. That action can ease dizziness and nausea before they build into vomiting.

The long duration shapes how meclizine fits into a trip. A full-day ferry crossing, a fishing charter or a day of open-water sailing can often be covered by a single dose. On a multiday cruise, it can be taken once a day rather than at shorter intervals. Travelers $TRV who would rather not track a dosing schedule while at sea may find that pattern simpler to manage.

The one-hour lead time matters as well. A tablet swallowed at the dock as the boat pulls away has less time to take effect before the vessel reaches rougher water. Taking it with breakfast, or about an hour before boarding, lines up with how the label describes its use.

Less sedating does not mean free of drowsiness. The meclizine label still warns that it may cause drowsiness. It adds that alcohol, sedatives and tranquilizers can increase that effect. The label also advises caution when driving or operating machinery. That warning applies to anyone planning to take the helm of a small boat or drive home after docking. Dry mouth is another side effect some users notice.

Bonine is sold as a chewable tablet. That format lets it be taken without a glass of water at hand.

Sea-Band wristbands press a small plastic stud against the P6 acupressure point on the inner wrist, offering a drug-free approach to seasickness. Evidence on whether they reduce nausea at sea is mixed, and they carry no guarantee of relief.

Each band is a loop of knitted elastic fabric with a rounded stud sewn into its inner side. Once the band is on, the stud sits against the skin and applies steady pressure. The bands are sold in pairs and worn on both wrists at once.

P6 comes from traditional Chinese medicine. Its Chinese name, Neiguan, is usually translated as "inner gate" or "inner pass." The letter P refers to the pericardium meridian, the line on which the point sits in acupuncture charts.

Placement decides whether the stud presses the intended spot. P6 lies on the inner forearm, about three finger widths above the main wrist crease. It sits between the two tendons that stand out along the center of the forearm when the hand is flexed. The stud should rest there, not on the crease or the wrist bone.

Wearers can also push on the stud with the opposite hand for short stretches when queasiness builds. The band holds the stud in place, so the extra pressure lands on the same point.

Research on P6 acupressure for motion sickness has produced mixed results. The bands are not established as a dependable seasickness treatment, and relief cannot be predicted for any one wearer. That uncertainty is the key caveat for anyone counting on them.

The bands contain no drug. They do not cause the drowsiness or dry mouth linked to many seasickness pills. For the same reason, they can be worn alongside medication. Some travelers use them as a backup measure without adding another dose.

Fit is the chief practical concern. A band that is too tight can leave the hand uncomfortable. It should feel snug rather than constricting. The fabric bands can be washed and reused across trips.

Ginger is a traditional nausea remedy that many travelers take at sea as capsules, chews or tea. Evidence for its effect on seasickness is mixed. Results for any one traveler are hard to predict. In food amounts, ginger is generally safe.

The ginger used for nausea comes from the rhizome, or underground stem, of the Zingiber officinale plant. It has a long history in cooking and traditional medicine across Asia. Today it is a common kitchen ingredient worldwide. Its sharp, warm bite comes from natural compounds called gingerols. Drying or heating the root converts some gingerols into related compounds called shogaols.

Travelers $TRV can choose among several forms. Capsules hold powdered dried ginger and pack easily. Chews and crystallized ginger are sweeter options that can be eaten in small pieces. Crystallized ginger is simmered in sugar syrup and coated with sugar, which adds sweetness and calories. Tea can be made by steeping fresh slices of the root in hot water or by using prepared tea bags. Each form delivers a different amount of ginger.

Product strength is worth checking on the label. In the U.S., ginger capsules are sold as dietary supplements, not approved drugs. The Food and Drug Administration does not review supplements for effectiveness before they go on sale. Strength and quality can differ from one brand to the next.

Ginger-flavored drinks are a separate case. Some commercial ginger ales rely on flavoring and contain little real ginger.

Ginger can have side effects of its own. Some people get mild stomach upset, such as heartburn or an unsettled stomach. Others report gas or diarrhea.

Ginger does not cure seasickness, and it does not work for everyone. For travelers with a history of severe motion sickness at sea, the mixed evidence means it is not a substitute for medication. For mild queasiness, it offers a drug-free choice that many people already keep in the kitchen.

Keeping your eyes on the horizon and staying low and near the middle of the vessel are two low-effort ways to ease seasickness. Both target the same problem: a mismatch between what the eyes see and what the inner ear senses.

The inner ear contains the vestibular system, which detects movement and changes in head position. On a moving boat, it registers every rise, fall and tilt of the deck.

The horizon gives the eyes a fixed outside reference. As the deck shifts beneath you, the line where sea meets sky stays put. What the eyes see then lines up more closely with what the inner ear feels. A distant coastline or a stationary landmark on shore can do the same job when haze or swell hides the horizon.

The same mismatch explains why reading, scrolling on a phone or working on a laptop at sea can make symptoms worse. Close focus on a page or screen holds the eyes on an object that rides along with the vessel.

Where you sit or stand matters too. A boat moves in several ways at once. Pitch is the rocking motion from bow to stern. Roll is the side-to-side tilt from one rail to the other. Both are smallest low in the hull and near the middle of the vessel. The bow and stern swing through the widest arc as the boat pitches. Higher decks sway farther as it rolls.

On a small boat, that usually means a seat near the center of the cockpit or deck. On a ferry or cruise ship, it points to lower decks and spaces near midship. For travelers booking a cabin on a longer voyage, a lower-deck room near the middle of the ship places the bed where the vessel moves least.

Fresh air on deck is also commonly advised. Being outside keeps the horizon in view. It also moves people away from stuffy interior rooms and strong odors such as engine exhaust, which can add to nausea.

A light meal before sailing, steady sips of water and no alcohol or heavy, greasy food make up the standard dietary advice for limiting seasickness. None of these steps prevents motion sickness by itself, but each one avoids adding extra strain to a stomach already unsettled by motion.

The light meal matters in both directions. A large, rich meal sits in the stomach for longer and gives nausea more to work with once a boat starts to pitch and roll. Setting out on a completely empty stomach is not the answer either, because hunger can make queasiness feel worse. Travelers $TRV commonly settle on something modest and plain, such as toast, crackers, rice or a banana, eaten some time before boarding.

Greasy and fried food is the category most often singled out to skip. Fatty meals take longer to digest than simple starches. Spicy dishes and strongly scented food are also commonly avoided. Cooking smells alone can push an uneasy passenger toward nausea.

Hydration is the second half of the advice. Small, frequent sips of water are easier on an unsettled stomach than gulping a large glass at once. Fluids matter more at sea than on land. Vomiting drains water and electrolytes from the body, and a dehydrated traveler tends to feel dizzier and more fatigued.

Alcohol works against both goals. It acts as a diuretic. That increases fluid loss and worsens dehydration. It also affects balance and can add to the dizziness that motion already causes. A drink at the bar on a cruise ship can compound the very symptoms a passenger is trying to avoid.

There is a medication angle as well. The labels for over-the-counter antihistamine motion sickness remedies warn that alcohol can increase drowsiness. That warning appears on both dimenhydrinate and meclizine products. Anyone combining those pills with drinks at sea is stacking two sedating effects.

Taking seasickness medication before symptoms start gives it the best chance to help. Oral tablets and the scopolamine patch tend to perform better when the drug is already in the body as the boat begins to move. Once queasiness takes hold, pills become harder to absorb.

The reason is practical. A pill swallowed by mouth has to pass through the stomach and be absorbed before it can act. Nausea slows that process. A traveler who is already queasy may also struggle to keep a tablet down. A pill brought back up soon after swallowing may not deliver its full dose.

The first dose is the one to plan. Each product carries its own instructions on how far ahead of departure to take it, and those directions differ from one medication to the next. Reading the package label, or the prescription instructions for a patch, before the day of the trip leaves room to plan around them.

The patch shows the logic clearly. It releases its drug through the skin over time, so it needs a head start before the boat leaves the dock. Applying it once the deck is already pitching gives it less chance to help during the roughest stretch.

Planning ahead also covers where the medication is stored. Pills packed deep in a checked bag or a cabin suitcase are hard to reach at the moment they are needed. Keeping them in a pocket or day bag makes it easier to take them on schedule, including any repeat doses the label allows on a longer outing.

Early dosing has a tradeoff. Several seasickness medications can cause drowsiness, and taking them in advance means that side effect may arrive before the trip begins. Travelers $TRV who will be driving to the port, or who need to stay alert on board, can raise that issue with a pharmacist when choosing a product.

Early dosing is not a guarantee. Some travelers still feel unwell after taking medication on schedule. Waiting until nausea has set in, though, leaves both pills and the patch less room to help.

Many people who feel sick at the start of a voyage adjust after a couple of days at sea. This process is known as habituation, and it is what sailors mean by finding their sea legs. It builds with repeated exposure to the motion of a boat.

Seasickness stems from a conflict between what the eyes see and what the inner ear senses. Habituation is the brain gradually learning to expect the movement of the deck. As those expectations update, the conflict produces fewer symptoms.

That is the basis of the familiar advice to give the body time. A rough first day does not mean the rest of a cruise or crossing will feel the same. The opening stretch of a voyage is often the hardest part.

The adjustment varies widely from person to person. Some travelers adapt quickly, while others take longer or never fully settle when conditions turn rough. No set timeline applies to everyone. Rougher water later in a trip can also bring symptoms back after a calm start.

Repeated exposure matters across trips, not just within one. People who spend time on the water regularly tend to build tolerance over time. Someone planning a long passage may find that shorter outings beforehand help the body grow used to motion.

Short trips work differently. A ferry crossing or a half-day fishing charter may end before adaptation has a chance to take hold. On those outings, options such as medication, a stable seat and careful food choices matter more.

The process can also run in reverse once a voyage ends. Some people feel a lingering sense of rocking after stepping back onto land, sometimes called land sickness. This is the same adaptation at work, as the brain readjusts to solid ground.

Habituation does not rule out other remedies. Travelers $TRV can use medication early in a trip and still adapt as the days pass.

Anyone with glaucoma, prostate problems or a pregnancy should check with a doctor or pharmacist before using scopolamine or antihistamines for seasickness. The same applies to anyone taking other medications. Persistent vomiting at sea is a separate warning sign that calls for medical attention.

Seasickness medication is easy to get, but that does not make it right for every traveler. The scopolamine patch requires a prescription, which builds in a conversation with a clinician. Dimenhydrinate and meclizine are sold over the counter. With those, the conversation happens only if the traveler starts it.

Existing health conditions are one common reason to ask. The scopolamine label warns that some users experience blurred vision and urinary retention. Those effects carry more weight for someone already managing an eye condition such as glaucoma. They also matter for someone with a prostate problem that affects urination. A doctor can judge whether the patch is a reasonable choice or whether another option fits better.

Other medications matter as well. Drowsiness is a known side effect of both scopolamine and the antihistamines used for motion sickness at sea. A pharmacist can review a full medication list and flag anything that could add to that sedation or otherwise interact. That review is best done before the trip, not after boarding.

Certain travelers face specific cautions on product labels. Children, older adults and pregnant travelers are each singled out. The guidance that applies to a healthy adult may not apply to them. Reading the full label and asking a pharmacist before the first dose is the conservative course for anyone in these groups.

Vomiting that does not let up is a clear sign to get help. Repeated vomiting drains the body of fluids, and dehydration can follow. Someone who cannot keep down water should seek medical care rather than wait for the motion to stop.

Dehydration brings its own symptoms, including dizziness and dry mouth. Those signs can overlap with ordinary seasickness. The overlap can make it hard for a traveler to tell when a rough crossing has turned into a medical problem.

Scientists tracked millions of phones to uncover patterns in carbon emissions

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Scientists used anonymized cell phone mobility data to estimate carbon emissions, testing the approach across four countries.

When COVID-19 lockdowns froze the planet in early 2020, carbon emissions plummeted almost overnight.

The problem? The instruments tracking those emissions couldn’t keep up.

Conventional inventories lag reality by months. Satellite inversions come with their own trade-offs between resolution and coverage.

A new study explores another approach: using people’s movements to help estimate carbon emissions.

The research was led by Mengning Wang and Xin Lu from China’s National University of Defense Technology.

Collaborators included Xiaoling Zhang from the University of Hong Kong and scholars from various international institutions.

The researchers turned anonymized cell phone signals from 318 million Chinese mobile users into what amounts to a near-real-time carbon meter.

They tested the framework across four countries.

Commuting, deliveries, business trips, and holiday travel often generate carbon emissions.

The researchers reasoned that if so many carbon-producing processes involve physically moving people and goods, then the movement itself might serve as a fast measuring stick.

They took anonymized signaling data from China Unicom and converted it into daily intercity mobility networks.

Each city became a node and each flow of people between cities became an edge.

From these networks they extracted structural features – how central a city is, how tightly clustered its connections are, how many links it maintains – and combined those with population and geographic data.

Then they trained machine learning models against the GRACED dataset, which tracks daily CO2 emissions across seven sectors: power, industry, residential consumption, ground transport, domestic aviation, international aviation, and shipping.

The window researchers studied was January and February 2020. It’s hard to imagine a harder test.

Spring Festival travel sent 68.74 million daily intercity trips surging through the network. Then lockdowns hit.

Mobility crashed by 71.3%. Multi-source carbon emissions dropped from an average of roughly 356.5 million kilograms of carbon per day to a minimum of 209.6 million – a 41.2% collapse. The whole thing happened in weeks.

And yet the correlation between daily national mobility and CO2 emissions held firm at r = 0.89. At the city level, it was a more modest but still significant r = 0.49.

The best-performing model – LightGBM, a tree-based ensemble – achieved R² values of 1.00 for multi-source emissions, 0.99 for domestic aviation, and 0.98 for ground transportation across 366 cities and 60 days.

Those are extraordinary numbers for something as messy as urban carbon output.

The real surprise came when the framework crossed borders.

Using open-source mobility datasets, the team applied the same approach to Italy, the continental United States, and Mexico.

The model reached R² values of 0.91, 0.95, and 0.99 respectively against the GRACED inventory.

Tested against a completely independent dataset – ODIAC, which derives emissions from nighttime light imagery and power-plant data at 1 km resolution – it still scored between 0.90 and 0.99.

Four countries, two independent benchmarks, consistent accuracy.

Something unexpected emerged from the mobility data. The Louvain community detection algorithm carved China’s 366 cities into 11 functional clusters.

These are groupings defined not by provincial borders but by how people actually move between them. Beijing-Tianjin-Hebei formed one cluster, pumping out 62.75 million kilograms of carbon daily.

The Yangtze River Delta formed another, at 49.71 million.

These clusters aren’t just descriptive. More central, more connected clusters emit more carbon.

But here’s the policy hook. China’s existing climate governance tends to target a handful of major emitting cities while overlooking smaller ones that sit in structurally critical positions within the network.

The mobility-defined clusters offer something different: a template for coordinated regional decarbonization that matches how economies actually function rather than how administrative maps happen to be drawn.

The authors aren’t overselling this. They call mobility a “partial but observable proxy” – a complement to inventories, not a replacement.

The Chinese analysis covers a single extraordinary two-month window. The data captures intercity movement, not what happens within a city.

Mobility alone may not fully capture emissions changes driven by seasonal energy demand, holiday schedules, or weekly cycles.

There are subtler problems too. Cellular signaling data inherits all the biases of telecom coverage – regional disparities, operator market share, sampling artifacts.

And the fundamental link between mobility and emissions could shift as remote work spreads, transport electrifies, and travel patterns reshape.

A correlation forged during the most dramatic behavioral disruption in modern history might not hold the same way during a quiet Tuesday in 2030.

Still, the core appeal is hard to argue with. Standard emissions inventories are accurate but glacially slow.

With timely access to mobility data, the framework could provide frequently updated emissions estimates.

If it scales, climate policymakers would gain a behavior-informed reading of the present rather than a months-old accounting of the past.

The team calls for fusing their mobility approach with remote sensing, energy structure data, and meteorological variables.

The ambition is to make carbon monitoring something closer to weather forecasting: imperfect, always updating, but fast enough to actually be useful.

The study is published in the journal The Innovation.

To Penny, or Not to Penny?

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Few shoes are as essential in the modern menswear landscape as the classic leather loafer. Whether you’re dressing up or dressing down, it is the “the Swiss Army Knife of shoes,” an any-occasion solution to many footwear conundrums. But within the broader loafer genus, one particular species—the penny loafer—includes a divisive design feature: a small, coin-shaped slot in the leather strap “saddle” across the vamp of the shoe.

As the lore goes, Ivy Style pioneers and sock-hopping teens in the mid-20th century liked to store a penny in their shoes as “payphone change.” But now that payphones are a thing of the misty-eyed past and the American one-cent penny is no longer minted for circulation, is it still cool to put a penny (or any other coin, or coin-shaped object) in your penny loafers? What is the line between timeless and passé?

We invited two of GQ’s most well-heeled staffers, associate grooming and wellness editor Tyler Chin and senior style commerce writer Gerald Ortiz, to ponder the pros and cons of the penny loafer.

Mahershala Ali, an ostensible penny proponent, in London on October 19.

Tyler Chin, associate grooming and wellness editor: Sure! I’ve seen so many guys wearing loafers these days, and yet still haven’t seen any of them harking back to the shoe’s origins with a penny in them. Maybe I’m not looking hard enough or I’m not in circles where they are doing that, but it’s an inoffensive and easy way to accessorize the shoe. Plus, I had an English teacher in high school who did it, and while she wasn’t particularly cool, I did think the sartorial choice was neat.

Gerald Ortiz, senior style commerce writer: No. I feel like guys who put pennies in their loafers are either try-hards, or just learned about that particular piece of prep trivia. The only acceptable instance to wear a penny in your loafers is if you’re really about that prep/Ivy style, like Preppy Pete, or the guys who frequent Alfargo’s Market (shout out).

TC: I think the penny would be the most boring and obvious choice. If a quarter could fit in there, I think it’d be so funny if you were at a vending machine and short a few cents and settled up with the coin in your shoe. One time, the button on my pants fell off on the street and I lost it, but imagine I had a spare button in my shoe? I could reattach it right there and then! In the hypothetical I’d also need to be toting around a sewing kit, but hey, I’d love to think I could be MacGyver in that scenario.

GO: It doesn’t have to be anything at all. But if you’re going to do it, I don’t see why it couldn’t be another coin or object. It could be a Chuck E. Cheese token. Whatever you put in the slot, it would still be wack.

TC: I just got back from a trip to Tokyo, and I had a lot of leftover yen coins. Two of them—the five and 50 yen—have a hole on them, which differentiates them from your boring American coin. I’d throw one of them in my shoe and be one of those people who never stops talking about their travel abroad.

GO: I saw on Instagram someone stick a hundred dollar bill into their loafer and that was funny for a second. Then I scrolled on to the next meme.

TC: I’m pretty sure a coin that is that close to the ground will surely have a patina before the end of the day. Also, I can’t remember the last time I’ve seen a shiny new coin—who carries loose change these days anyway?!

GO: You know, it might be kind of cool to see a penny completely covered in verdigris slotted into your loafers. But maybe the coin should match the sheen of your loafers. I dunno, just don’t do it and you won’t have to think about it.

TC: Everyone I know is very familiar with my love of penny loafers. Lately I’ve been wearing them with bootcut jeans that are just a little longer than usual, so they have a nice drape over the shoe. Top-wise, I’d go with a tucked-in button up, T-shirt, or sweater. Pretty much anything that would go well with sneakers would be equally suited for wearing with loafers. Which is nearly all outfits.

GO: Penniless or not, a pair of wide khakis with a clean-to-slight break and a striped button-up is a failsafe outfit. It wouldn’t be very inventive, but it would pass muster for most conceivable situations. Vintage straight-leg jeans with good fades, a fucked-up band tee, and a sportcoat is also a great look. But the best looks are always the unexpected pairings.

TC: It’s sort of like how guys will go on and on about having functional sleeve buttons on a suit jacket. Is there any situation where you’ll actually need to unbutton those or do you just leave one or two undone to “stunt” on your unimpressed bros? If you really want to put a coin in your shoe for the sake of accessorizing your footwear, then by all means get a shoe with a functioning penny slot and use it! But if you’re doing it just so you can say “Yeah, these joints are the real deal!” and use it as an excuse to talk about the history of the shoe and why yours are superior, then I’d rather you wear lace-ups.

GO: I think it’s fine, even preferable to have a faux penny slot. It’s like how jeans still come with the watch pocket. It’s purely vestigial at this point, but would feel incomplete without it.

TC: One of these days, I’ll be telling my great-great grandchildren about the origins of the penny loafer and they’ll be confused because they would’ve never heard or seen the one-cent coin before. I’ll whip one out of my shoe and instead of being impressed they’ll chalk it up to another one of Old Man Ty’s tales from his youth, and tuck me back into the nursing home they checked me into.

GO: Lol. I guess it’ll make the penny more covetable. But it’ll be a long time before the penny becomes that desirable. That said, I love this British brand Six 95 that’s designing cool pieces using coins, so I can absolutely see more labels farming pennies for their clothes and accessories.

TC: Men will break up anything other than a toxic relationship. Loafers did nothing wrong and sometimes it’s just the person wearing them that needs to hit the curb.

GO: It makes sense. It’s like any trend, really. Even if they’re as versatile as we’ve been propping them up to be, loafers reached an inflection point where, for some guys, they’re just not the vibe anymore. But I don’t think they’ll dip so low to be thought of as cringe or as an emblem of the 2020s. They’re as classic as it gets and they’ll go through their own ebbs and flows.

TC: A quarter to increase my net worth 25-fold.

Where to Eat During AFM 2026: Century City and Beverly Hills’ Best Restaurants

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

AFM 2026 is in the center of the independent film business dealmaking world and close to many of the entertainment biz’s favorite restaurants. Century City and nearby Beverly Hills are home to the major agencies, law firms and financing entities, which equals power lunching and see-and-be-seen gathering spots close to the confab. Throw in some legendary eateries, and AFMers are perfectly positioned to dine where Hollywood dealmakers, agency foot soldiers and bold names eat out.Century City’s dining options start at the host venue: Birdie G’s is the Fairmont Century Plaza’s new full-service restaurant. Expect classic American cooking in a relaxed setting. There will be mobile coffee stations and daily grab-and-go items near the Fairmont’s lower-level conference rooms. Choices include hearty breakfast burritos, creative salads and an array of sandwiches.At the adjacent Westfield Century City, where AFM badge holders can take advantage of a discount program, more than 65 food outlets populate the multi-level, open-air mall.Specializing in dim sum, Din Tai Fung (10250 Santa Monica Blvd., second level, dtf.com) excels in Taiwan-style, handmade soup dumplings and flavorful noodle dishes. Waits can be extensive, so sit at the bar, reserve ahead or use yelp.com to join the real-time waitlist. Related Stories Pixar Names Jonathan Garson as President; Jim Morris Moves to Emeritus Role

Terra, the restaurant at Eataly Los Angeles (10250 Santa Monica Blvd., Eataly.com), boasts ample outdoor seating to enjoy Terra’s wood-fired Italian dishes and craft cocktails. The Monday-Friday happy hour on the bar terrace features $10 glasses of wine, cocktails and Aperol spritzes paired with grilled appetizers.

On the lower level of the nearby Century Building, Hinoki & the Bird (10 W. Century Dr., hinokiandthebird.com) attracts the high-powered lunch crowd, including CAA agents along with their clients and execs from the Fox studio lot. Serving globally inspired California cuisine, lunch favorites range from sashimi starters to the ever-popular crispy jidori chicken to the kale salad topped with spiced cashews. Enticing dinner entrees include the hinoki-scented black cod and dry-aged Peking duck. The tucked-away location provides privacy; the sheltered, heated outdoor patio is open year-round. Reservations are highly recommended. Monday-Friday happy hour runs 5-7 p.m.

Beverly Hills excels at chic hotel dining. Opened in May, Gemma on the rooftop of the Waldorf Astoria Beverly Hills (9850 Wilshire Blvd., waldorfastoriabeverlyhills.com) has unequaled views across Beverly Hills. The upscale pan-Asian fare (highlights: dim sum, seafood raw bar, lobster burger and Singaporean noodles) and fanciful cocktails more than satisfy the rooftop’s stylish crowd. Edoardo Baldi’s Tuscan steakhouse Baldi (baldi.com) brings the restaurateur’s signature hospitality and famed pasta to the Waldorf’s first floor.

The Maybourne Beverly Hills (225 N. Canon Dr., maybournebeverlyhills.com) boasts a caviar and oyster bar at its cozy Maybourne Bar. Tradition and old-school service reign on the leafy patio of the Beverly Hills Hotel’s Polo Lounge (9641 Sunset Blvd., dorchestercollection.com), a go-to for Hollywood’s elite for decades.

Mastro’s is celebrating 25 years, and the Garden at the Penthouse at Mastro’s Steakhouse Beverly Hills (246 Canon Dr., mastrosrestaurants.com) welcomes power players like Michael B. Jordan, Emily Blunt and John Krasinski to its clubby third-floor indoor-outdoor dining room. For dinner, there are massive steaks and chops and a heady assortment of seafood.

For those seeking a flowery, photo-ready indoor-outdoor space that feels miles away, take the world’s prettiest elevator to the rooftop restaurant Monsieur Dior by Dominique Crenn (323 N. Rodeo Dr., monsieurdiorbeverlyhills.com). The inventive lunch, dinner and lounge menus come from Michelin-starred restaurateur/chef Dominique Crenn, paired with elegant interiors by architect Peter Marino. Sip on a glass of Chateau Minuty, the house rosé wine from Provence, France, to embrace the French ambiance.

The businessperson’s lunch crowd flocks to Avra Beverly Hills (233 N. Beverly Dr., theavragroup.com) for the prix fixe, three-course Mediterranean-style lunch special priced at $41.95. Start with a Greek salad, then move on to the grilled salmon and then finish with a baklava, all served in Avra’s bright, open-air setting populated by the city’s elite.

Latin Grammy Awards 2026 Predictions: Who Will Win the Top Honors?

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Billboard's Latin and Español editors offer an analysis of the likely winners and deserving contenders across the top four categories.

Who will win the top prize at the 2026 Latin Grammys? Every year, Billboard Latin and Billboard Español editors dissect the top categories — album of the year, song of the year, record of the year and best new artist — to forecast the winners and spotlight the most deserving artists.

With the ceremony just around the corner this Thursday (Nov. 12), our editorial team has engaged in an analysis, considering market trends and historical voting patterns to make informed predictions. This discussion includes insights from Sigal Ratner-Arias, deputy editor of Billboard Español; Jessica Roiz, associate editor for Latin; and Ingrid Fajardo, senior social media manager and staff writer.

On its 26th annual edition, the Latin Grammy Awards ceremony will broadcast live from the MGM Grand Garden Arena in Las Vegas. The three-hour telecast will be produced by TelevisaUnivision and air across the network’s U.S. platforms, beginning at 8 p.m. ET. It will be preceded by the Latin Grammy Premiere, where the majority of the categories are awarded.

To refresh your memory, here’s this year’s full list of nominees. Below are our predictions for the general categories:

Jessica Roiz: All are great works, but the clear winner in my book is Milo J’s La Vida Era Más Corta. In 15 songs, the then 18-year-old bridged the past, present and future, most notably with his star-studded collaborations that include Trueno, Akriila, Silvio Rodríguez and the late Mercedes Sosa. Showcasing the maturity beyond his years, the Argentine artist — who’s one of the youngest to be nominated in this coveted category — beautifully captures Latin America’s rich folk traditions, where chacareras, zambas, chamamés and tangos effortlessly coexist.

Sigal Ratner-Arias: I agree that all of these are excellent and that Milo — despite his young age — has created a truly extraordinary album. However, I don’t see any other winner here besides Rosalía for LUX, a complex, one-of-a-kind masterpiece. Recorded with the London Symphony Orchestra and featuring songs musically and lyrically rich — in 13 different languages! — Rosalía not only expanded the meaning of what a pop album can be, she also reached new vocal heights with an impressive operatic work.

Ingrid Fajardo: I agree with Jessica and Sigal; we definitely saw masterpieces in this category, including Milo J’s La Vida Era Más Corta and Carín León’s MUDA. But Rosalía took us into her world with LUX. For me, it’s a work of art; it feels like reaching the deepest part of her musical thoughts, her knowledge and the art she carries inside, captured in this 18-track album combining a masterful symphony orchestra perfectly intertwined with pop that makes you feel the performance of each song.

Jessica Roiz: Traditionally, the song of the year honors the musical and lyrical composition of a song, which is why I would love to see “Coleccionando Heridas” by Karol G and Marco Antonio Solís nab the award this year. There’s something about the nostalgia aspect of this song that has me in a chokehold, from its grupero love ballad melody to its heartfelt and relatable lyrics. “Coleccionando Heridas” (collecting wounds) puts on the forefront the modern-day dating life that can be quite challenging.

Sigal Ratner-Arias: “Coleccionando Heridas” is also one of my favorites on this list, and if it wins, it would be well-deserved, no doubt. However, I think Rosalía could easily take home this award with “La Perla” alongside Yahritza y Su Esencia. The song, with its brilliantly ironic lyrics about a manipulative ex-partner, has become an anthem of catharsis, also driven by an exhilarating orchestral arrangement and the powerful voices of Rosi and Yahritza.

Ingrid Fajardo: This is a tough category, as many nominees tackle more traditional themes in their lyrics. For example, Karol G’s “Coleccionando Heridas” delivers a direct message about love and heartbreak, while Rosalía’s “La Perla”, which could be described as “communal therapy,” brilliantly captures the characteristics of a narcissistic person. Milo J’s “Solifican12″ stands out with its folk-inspired sound and introspective verses, using metaphor to explore personal transformation. For its depth and originality, I’d love to see “Solifican12” take home the Latin Grammy.

Roiz: Every song in this category is a true masterpiece, but I’m personally torn between two songs that are complete opposites, but impactful in their own right. The very motivational “Alma” not only introduced an edgy, confident and fearless side to Elena Rose, but its acoustic and minimalist soundscape truly redefined today’s Latin pop music scene. Meanwhile, Rosalía’s “La Perla” featuring Yahritza y Su Esencia is an elegant melting pot that laces ranchera, classical symphony and waltz music, backed by the power vocals of the Spanish singer-songwriter and the Mexican band’s lead Yahrtiza Martinez. Now, given the fact that the record of the year recognizes the overall audio recording and sonic performance of a track, I highly believe “La Perla” can take the Latin Grammy award this year.

Ratner-Arias: I love all the songs and artists in this category, but again, I feel that the award here will come down to “La Perla” and “Coleccionando Heridas.” For the same reasons I explained before, I’m leaning toward Rosalía taking this one as well.

Ingrid Fajardo: For me, it’s definitely Rosalía’s “La Perla,” featuring Yahritza y Su Esencia. The song opens with a gentle, traditional guitar, setting an intimate atmosphere that draws the listener in. As the track unfolds, violins gradually layer in, their melodies weaving around the vocals and deepening the emotional resonance. Initially, there’s a light, playful energy to the arrangement, but as the song progresses, the orchestration projects, and a full symphony emerges, infusing the piece with an unexpected intensity and dramatic power.

Roiz: Macario Martínez. There’s something so heartwarming and honest in his artistry that I would be very excited if he won best new artist. At 25 years old, the Mexican folk artist is an old-soul whose music is rooted in intimate lyrics and acoustic melodies, backed by his tender-yet-soulful vocals. What makes Martínez an even more charming and compelling artist is his success story from working as a street sweeper in Mexico to landing his first Latin Grammy nomination. Winning would only give more power to the viral track that got him here today: “Sueña Lindo, Corazón.” Talk about a full-circle moment.

Ratner-Arias: I agree that it would be wonderful if Macario won — and well-deserved, too. That said, it’s been a long time since I’ve seen this much excitement in the industry surrounding one new artist — and that is Aria Vega. Our Latin Up-And-Comer for the month of July has stood out this year in a way few debut artists do, connecting with audiences not only through her refreshing fusion of urban and Caribbean rhythms but also through her infectious joy and unmatched charisma. I believe this costeñita from Barranquilla is well on her way to becoming a true superstar, and is the one to beat here.

Ingrid Fajardo: Aria is truly having a remarkable year. In February 2026, I featured her on Billboard‘s On the Radar Latin, and just five months later she was named our Latin Up-And-Comer. Audiences have genuinely connected to her essence and the fresh perspective she brings to Latin music. But I also want to highlight Macario Martínez, whose journey is a true testament to the power of dreams. He began with a viral video, just him, his music, and a heartfelt plea for listeners’ support. What started as a simple, authentic moment blossomed into a story with a happy ending. Throughout his rise, Macario has stayed true to his roots, maintaining a sound that feels like that of an old soul, someone who has returned to share his music and allow others to see the world through his eyes. In many ways, he reminds me of Juan Gabriel through his music, carrying his Mexican heritage with a very nostalgic sound.

Credit card debt climbs to a record $1.29 trillion, New York Fed research finds

📅 2026-11-10  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Americans are increasingly turning to credit cards as financial pressures mount, new reports show.

Collectively, credit card balances climbed by $22 billion in the third quarter to a record $1.29 trillion, according to the Federal Reserve Bank of New York's latest household debt report, released Tuesday. Balances are up 4.2% from a year earlier.

Total household debt, including mortgages, auto loans and student loans, also expanded by $334 billion for the July-to-September period to a record $19.1 trillion.

"Overall debt has been growing," the New York Fed researchers said on a press call Tuesday. "This does reflect financial hardship out there."

The average credit card balance per consumer now stands at $6,678, up 2.4% year over year, a separate quarterly credit industry insights report from TransUnion found. The number of people with credit cards rose 2% to 178 million from 174.8 million.

Consumers often turn to credit cards in part because they are more accessible than other types of loans — even though the average interest rate on a new credit card offer is about 24%, according to data from LendingTree. Credit card APRs vary depending on the type of card, the issuer and the borrower's creditworthiness.

"Credit cards continue to be one of the most expensive ways to borrow," said Charlie Wise, TransUnion's senior vice president of global research and consulting.

Over time, an increasing share of new credit cards have gone to borrowers with lower incomes and credit scores, and "those accounts are now showing higher levels of distress," the New York Fed researchers said in a corresponding blog post.

After credit card issuers loosened their lending standards during the Covid pandemic, delinquency rates rose in the years that followed, the New York Fed researchers said on the press call.

"Macroeconomic headwinds — namely high inflation and rising borrowing costs — have squeezed consumers, with inflation outpacing wage growth for low-income brackets," according to the New York Fed.

Meanwhile, the cost of living continues to climb, with Americans paying more for essentials such as groceries and gas.

Stubborn inflation has now stretched on for years, putting many households in an affordability squeeze. Costs for goods and services rose more than expected again in October, the Bureau of Labor Statistics also reported Tuesday.

"Higher prices and higher rates have pushed more people into debt," said Ted Rossman, principal consumer finance analyst at Money Management International, a nonprofit credit counseling agency.

Balances have continued to swell in the wake of the pandemic, and even though the growth rate is now slowing, "from a household finance standpoint, a lot of damage has been done," Rossman said.

The reports come on the heels of the Democrats' strong midterm results, when many voters expressed their dissatisfaction with the U.S. economy, according to Kyle Moore, chief economist at the nonpartisan think tank The Century Foundation.

"The throughline connecting so many of Tuesday's outcomes was a recognition that the American economy is failing American families," Moore said, referring to Election Day.
